# Mock Exam: Lecture 4, Wheel Odometry

**Time:** 30 minutes | **Total:** 20 marks | **Answer all questions.**
Give answers to 3 significant figures unless told otherwise. Angles are in radians.

---

**Question 1** [3 marks]

(a) A wheel of radius *r* turns through an angle Δφ. Write an expression for the distance it travels along the ground. **[1]**

(b) The pose update uses θ + Δθ/2 rather than θ inside the sine and cosine. Explain why. **[2]**

---

**Question 2** [6 marks]

A differential-drive robot has wheel radius r = 0.05 m and wheelbase L = 0.40 m. It starts at pose (x, y, θ) = (0, 0, 0). In one time step the encoders report Δφ_L = 2.0 rad and Δφ_R = 2.4 rad.

(a) Calculate ΔS_L and ΔS_R. **[1]**

(b) Calculate ΔS and Δθ. **[2]**

(c) Calculate the new pose (x, y, θ). **[2]**

(d) What would y be if the update used the heading at the start of the step instead of the mean heading? **[1]**

---

**Question 3** [5 marks]

Both wheels of a robot are believed to have radius 0.050 m, but the right wheel actually has radius 0.0505 m. The wheelbase is L = 0.40 m. The robot is told to drive 1 m in a straight line, so each wheel turns through exactly 20 rad.

(a) Calculate the robot's **actual** change in heading over this motion. What heading change does odometry report? **[3]**

(b) The robot then drives a further 10 m. Estimate the extra position error caused by this heading error alone. **[1]**

(c) State what the notes say about how odometry error grows over time, and give the reason. **[1]**

---

**Question 4** [3 marks]

(a) Classify each of these as a **systematic** or **non-systematic** source of odometry error: **[2]**
 i. Wheel slip on a wet floor
 ii. Finite encoder resolution
 iii. A wheelbase L that was measured wrongly
 iv. Unequal wheel radii

(b) Explain why wheel slip cannot be calibrated out. **[1]**

---

**Question 5** [3 marks]

(a) Briefly describe the path the robot drives in the UMBmark calibration method. **[1]**

(b) Why does UMBmark drive the path both clockwise **and** anticlockwise, instead of in one direction only? **[2]**

---
---

# Mark Scheme

**Q1**
- (a) ΔS = r·Δφ **[1]**
- (b) The robot turns during the step **[1]**, so the mean heading over the step (θ + Δθ/2) estimates the direction of travel better than the heading at the start **[1]**.

**Q2**
- (a) ΔS_L = 0.05 × 2.0 = **0.100 m**; ΔS_R = 0.05 × 2.4 = **0.120 m** **[1]**
- (b) ΔS = (0.120 + 0.100)/2 = **0.110 m** **[1]**; Δθ = (0.120 − 0.100)/0.40 = **0.0500 rad** **[1]**
- (c) Mean heading is 0.025 rad.
 x = 0.110·cos(0.025) = **0.110 m** (0.10997)
 y = 0.110·sin(0.025) = **0.00275 m** (2.75 mm) **[1 for x and y]**
 θ = **0.0500 rad** **[1]**
- (d) y = 0.110·sin(0) = **0**. The start-heading version misses the 2.75 mm sideways motion. **[1]**

**Q3**
- (a) Actual distances: ΔS_R = 0.0505 × 20 = 1.010 m and ΔS_L = 0.050 × 20 = 1.000 m **[1]**
 Actual Δθ = (1.010 − 1.000)/0.40 = **0.025 rad** (about 1.4°) **[1]**
 Odometry reports **0 rad**, because both encoders show the same angle **[1]**
- (b) Error ≈ d·e = 10 × 0.025 = **0.25 m** **[1]**
- (c) The error is **unbounded** and grows with distance travelled, because each step adds error and nothing ever removes it. **[1]** (Both parts are needed for the mark.)

**Q4**
- (a) i. non-systematic; ii. systematic; iii. systematic; iv. systematic **[½ each]**
- (b) Slip is unpredictable and depends on conditions such as the floor surface. It has no fixed, repeatable bias that a correction could remove. **[1]**

**Q5**
- (a) The robot drives a square path. **[1]**
- (b) Unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase both produce end-position errors. Driving in both directions gives two different end-position errors **[1]**, and comparing them lets you separate the effect of each source. One direction alone can't tell them apart. **[1]**

---

Spending about 1.5 minutes per mark gets you through in 30 minutes. Q2 and Q3 carry the most marks, so practise those calculations until they're quick.

# Mock Exam: Lecture 4, Wheel Odometry

**Time:** 30 minutes. **Total:** 20 marks. Answer all questions. Give angles in radians unless a question asks otherwise.

---

**Question 1** (3 marks)

A differential-drive robot has wheel radius *r* and wheelbase *L*.

(a) Write expressions for the distance travelled by the robot's centre, ΔS, and the change in heading, Δθ, in terms of the wheel distances ΔS_L and ΔS_R. **[2]**

(b) What does it mean for the robot if ΔS_L = −ΔS_R? **[1]**

---

**Question 2** (5 marks)

A robot has wheel radius *r* = 0.05 m and wheelbase *L* = 0.40 m. It starts at pose (x, y, θ) = (0, 0, 0). In one time step the left wheel turns by 10 rad and the right wheel turns by 12 rad.

(a) Calculate ΔS_L, ΔS_R, ΔS and Δθ. **[2]**

(b) Calculate the new pose (x, y, θ). Use the half-angle update and give your answer to 3 significant figures. **[2]**

(c) Explain why the update uses θ + Δθ/2 and not θ. **[1]**

---

**Question 3** (4 marks)

(a) Explain why odometry position error is described as *unbounded*. **[1]**

(b) A robot's heading estimate is wrong by 1°. Estimate the resulting position error after it drives a further 10 m in a straight line. **[1]**

(c) The value of *L* used in the robot's software is 2% larger than the true wheelbase. The robot actually turns 90° on the spot and then drives 5 m straight. Estimate the heading error, in degrees, and the position error at the end of the 5 m. **[2]**

---

**Question 4** (4 marks)

(a) Classify each of the following as a **systematic** or **non-systematic** error source:
  i. the left wheel is slightly worn and smaller than the right
  ii. the wheels slip on a wet floor
  iii. the encoders have 512 counts per revolution
  iv. the wheelbase in the software was measured carelessly **[2]**

(b) Explain why wheel slip can't be removed by calibration but unequal wheel radii can. **[2]**

---

**Question 5** (4 marks)

(a) Describe the path and procedure used in the UMBmark calibration method. **[2]**

(b) Why does UMBmark drive the path in **both** directions rather than one? **[2]**

---
---

## Mark Scheme

**Q1**
(a) ΔS = (ΔS_R + ΔS_L)/2 **[1]**; Δθ = (ΔS_R − ΔS_L)/L **[1]**
(b) ΔS = 0, so the robot rotates on the spot with Δθ = 2ΔS_R/L. **[1]**

**Q2**
(a) ΔS_L = 0.05 × 10 = 0.50 m, ΔS_R = 0.05 × 12 = 0.60 m **[1]**. ΔS = 0.55 m, Δθ = 0.10/0.40 = 0.25 rad **[1]**
(b) Mean heading = 0.125 rad.
x = 0.55 cos(0.125) ≈ **0.546 m**, y = 0.55 sin(0.125) ≈ **0.0686 m** **[1]**, θ = **0.25 rad** **[1]**
(c) The robot turns during the step, so the average heading over the step estimates the direction of travel better than the heading at the start. **[1]** (Without the half-angle term the result would be (0.55, 0, 0.25), which misses about 7 cm in y.)

**Q3**
(a) Each step adds some error, and odometry has no way of correcting it, so the error builds up with distance travelled and has no upper limit. **[1]**
(b) 1° = 0.0175 rad, and 10 × 0.0175 ≈ **0.17 m** **[1]**
(c) Δθ_est = Δθ_true / 1.02, so the robot believes it turned 90/1.02 ≈ 88.2°. The heading error is therefore **≈ 1.8°** (0.031 rad) **[1]**. Position error ≈ 5 × 0.031 ≈ **0.16 m** **[1]**

**Q4**
(a) i systematic, ii non-systematic, iii systematic, iv systematic **[2]** (½ mark each)
(b) Systematic errors are consistent and repeatable, so they can be measured once and corrected, for example with a scale factor on one wheel **[1]**. Slip depends on the surface and conditions and changes unpredictably from moment to moment, so no fixed correction can remove it **[1]**.

**Q5**
(a) The robot drives a square path, once clockwise and once anticlockwise **[1]**. The end-position error (the gap between the final and starting positions) is measured for each run **[1]**.
(b) Unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase both leave an end-position error, and in a single direction their effects are mixed together and can partly cancel **[1]**. Their effects combine differently in the two directions, so comparing the two errors lets you separate them and correct each one **[1]**.
✗ fail grounded ×2

# Mock Exam: Lecture 4, Wheel Odometry

**Time: 30 minutes. Total: 20 marks. Answer all questions.**
Use radians throughout. Give numerical answers to 3 significant figures.

---

**Question 1** (3 marks)

(a) The pose update uses `theta + dtheta/2` rather than `theta` inside the cos and sin terms. Explain why. **[2]**

(b) In `dtheta = (dS_R − dS_L) / L`, what happens to the computed heading change if the wheelbase L is made larger while the wheel distances stay the same? **[1]**

---

**Question 2** (6 marks)

A differential-drive robot has wheel radius r = 0.05 m and wheelbase L = 0.40 m. It starts at pose (x, y, θ) = (0, 0, 0). In one time step the encoders report dφ_L = 2.0 rad and dφ_R = 2.4 rad.

(a) Calculate dS_L and dS_R. **[1]**

(b) Calculate dS and dθ. **[2]**

(c) Calculate the new pose (x, y, θ). **[2]**

(d) A student leaves out the half-angle term and uses θ at the start of the step. What y value do they get, and how far off is it from your answer to (c)? **[1]**

---

**Question 3** (4 marks)

(a) Explain why odometry error is described as *unbounded*. **[1]**

(b) A robot's heading estimate is wrong by 2°. It then drives a further 5 m in a straight line. Estimate the resulting position error. **[2]**

(c) Explain why heading error is considered the most damaging kind of odometry error. **[1]**

---

**Question 4** (3 marks)

(a) Classify each of the following as a **systematic** or **non-systematic** error source: **[2]**
 i. finite encoder resolution
 ii. wheel slip
 iii. unequal wheel radii
 iv. inaccurate wheelbase

(b) Explain why wheel slip cannot be removed by calibration. **[1]**

---

**Question 5** (4 marks)

(a) The robot from Question 2 actually has L = 0.40 m, but its software has been configured with L = 0.42 m. For the same encoder readings as Question 2, what heading change does the software compute, and what is the heading error for that step? **[2]**

(b) Describe the UMBmark calibration procedure, and explain why the robot is driven in *both* directions. **[2]**

---
---

# Mark Scheme

**Q1**
(a) The robot turns continuously during the step, so its heading changes from θ to θ + dθ **[1]**. The average heading, θ + dθ/2, is a better estimate of the direction it actually travelled than the heading at the start **[1]**.
(b) dθ gets smaller, because the same wheel-distance difference produces less rotation on a wider axle **[1]**.

**Q2**
(a) dS_L = 0.05 × 2.0 = **0.100 m**. dS_R = 0.05 × 2.4 = **0.120 m** **[1]**
(b) dS = (0.120 + 0.100)/2 = **0.110 m** **[1]**. dθ = (0.120 − 0.100)/0.40 = **0.0500 rad** **[1]**
(c) The mean heading is 0.025 rad.
 x = 0.110 × cos(0.025) = **0.110 m** (0.10997)
 y = 0.110 × sin(0.025) = **0.00275 m**
 θ = **0.0500 rad**
 **[1]** for using the correct mean heading, **[1]** for correct values.
(d) With θ = 0, y = 0.110 × sin(0) = **0**. This is off by about **0.00275 m (2.75 mm)** **[1]**.

**Q3**
(a) Every step adds a little error and nothing ever corrects it, so the error keeps growing with the distance travelled **[1]**.
(b) e = 2° × π/180 = 0.0349 rad **[1]**. Position error ≈ d × e = 5 × 0.0349 ≈ **0.175 m** **[1]**.
(c) A heading error doesn't stay fixed. It turns into a position error that keeps growing (≈ d·e) with every further metre driven **[1]**.

**Q4**
(a) i. systematic, ii. non-systematic, iii. systematic, iv. systematic. **[½ each]**
(b) Slip is random and depends on things like the floor surface. It is not a repeatable property of the robot, so there is no fixed correction you can measure and apply **[1]**.

**Q5**
(a) Computed dθ = 0.020/0.42 = **0.0476 rad** **[1]**. The true value is 0.0500 rad, so the heading error is about **0.0024 rad** per step, an underestimate. Because this is systematic, it builds up on every turn **[1]**.
(b) The robot is driven around a square path both clockwise and anticlockwise, and the end-position error is measured each time **[1]**. Two runs give two measurements, which is what you need to separate the two unknowns: unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase **[1]**.
 *(Beyond the notes, for credit if mentioned: the two errors can cancel each other out in one direction and add up in the other, so a single-direction test could look misleadingly accurate.)*

---

**Suggested timing:** about 1.5 minutes per mark, which leaves around 2 minutes to check your work. Q2 is the longest question, so aim to finish it by minute 12.

**Questions on the notes themselves:**
- 4.3 says heading error becomes a position error of "about d × e". That only holds for small angles (sin e ≈ e), so the 2° in Q3 is fine. Check whether your lecturer expects you to state that assumption.
- 4.5 doesn't say *why* both directions are needed. If that could be examined, look up the full explanation in your course reading.
✗ fail grounded ×2

(b) dθ gets smaller, because the same wheel-distance difference produces less rotation on a wider axle **[1]**.

**Q2**
(a) dS_L = 0.05 × 2.0 = **0.100 m**. dS_R = 0.05 × 2.4 = **0.120 m** **[1]**
(b) dS = (0.120 + 0.100)/2 = **0.110 m** **[1]**. dθ = (0.120 − 0.100)/0.40 = **0.0500 rad** **[1]**
(c) The mean heading is 0.025 rad.
 x = 0.110 × cos(0.025) = **0.110 m** (0.10997)
 y = 0.110 × sin(0.025) = **0.00275 m**
 θ = **0.0500 rad**
 **[1]** for using the correct mean heading, **[1]** for correct values.
(d) With θ = 0, y = 0.110 × sin(0) = **0**. This is off by about **0.00275 m (2.75 mm)** **[1]**.

**Q3**
(a) Every step adds a little error and nothing ever corrects it, so the error keeps growing with the distance travelled **[1]**.
(b) e = 2° × π/180 = 0.0349 rad **[1]**. Position error ≈ d × e = 5 × 0.0349 ≈ **0.175 m** **[1]**.
(c) A heading error doesn't stay fixed. It turns into a position error that keeps growing (≈ d·e) with every further metre driven **[1]**.

**Q4**
(a) i. systematic, ii. non-systematic, iii. systematic, iv. systematic. **[½ each]**
(b) Slip is random and depends on things like the floor surface. It is not a repeatable property of the robot, so there is no fixed correction you can measure and apply **[1]**.

**Q5**
(a) Computed dθ = 0.020/0.42 = **0.0476 rad** **[1]**. The true value is 0.0500 rad, so the heading error is about **0.0024 rad** per step, an underestimate. Because this is systematic, it builds up on every turn **[1]**.
(b) The robot is driven around a square path both clockwise and anticlockwise, and the end-position error is measured each time **[1]**. Two runs give two measurements, which is what you need to separate the two unknowns: unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase **[1]**.
 *(Beyond the notes, for credit if mentioned: the two errors can cancel each other out in one direction and add up in the other, so a single-direction test could look misleadingly accurate.)*

---

**Suggested timing:** about 1.5 minutes per mark, which leaves around 2 minutes to check your work. Q2 is the longest question, so aim to finish it by minute 12.

**Questions on the notes themselves:**
- 4.3 says heading error becomes a position error of "about d × e". That only holds for small angles (sin e ≈ e), so the 2° in Q3 is fine. Check whether your lecturer expects you to state that assumption.
- 4.5 doesn't say *why* both directions are needed. If that could be examined, look up the full explanation in your course reading.

# Mock Exam: Lecture 4, Wheel Odometry

**Time: 30 minutes. Total: 20 marks. Answer all questions.**
Use radians throughout. Give numerical answers to 3 significant figures.

---

**Question 1** (3 marks)

(a) The pose update uses `theta + dtheta/2` rather than `theta` inside the cos and sin terms. Explain why. **[2]**

(b) In `dtheta = (dS_R − dS_L) / L`, what happens to the computed heading change if the wheelbase L is made larger while the wheel distances stay the same? **[1]**

---

**Question 2** (6 marks)

A differential-drive robot has wheel radius r = 0.05 m and wheelbase L = 0.40 m. It starts at pose (x, y, θ) = (0, 0, 0). In one time step the encoders report dφ_L = 2.0 rad and dφ_R = 2.4 rad.

(a) Calculate dS_L and dS_R. **[1]**

(b) Calculate dS and dθ. **[2]**

(c) Calculate the new pose (x, y, θ). **[2]**

(d) A student leaves out the half-angle term and uses θ at the start of the step. What y value do they get, and how far off is it from your answer to (c)? **[1]**

---

**Question 3** (4 marks)

(a) Explain why odometry error is described as *unbounded*. **[1]**

(b) A robot's heading estimate is wrong by 2°. It then drives a further 5 m in a straight line. Estimate the resulting position error. **[2]**

(c) Explain why heading error is considered the most damaging kind of odometry error. **[1]**

---

**Question 4** (3 marks)

(a) Classify each of the following as a **systematic** or **non-systematic** error source: **[2]**
 i. finite encoder resolution
 ii. wheel slip
 iii. unequal wheel radii
 iv. inaccurate wheelbase

(b) Explain why wheel slip cannot be removed by calibration. **[1]**

---

**Question 5** (4 marks)

(a) The robot from Question 2 actually has L = 0.40 m, but its software has been configured with L = 0.42 m. For the same encoder readings as Question 2, what heading change does the software compute, and what is the heading error for that step? **[2]**

(b) Describe the UMBmark calibration procedure, and explain why the robot is driven in *both* directions. **[2]**

---
---

# Mark Scheme

**Q1**
(a) The robot turns continuously during the step, so its heading changes from θ to θ + dθ **[1]**. The average heading, θ + dθ/2, is a better estimate of the direction it actually travelled than the heading at the start **[1]**.
(b) dθ gets smaller, because the same wheel-distance difference produces less rotation on a wider axle **[1]**.

**Q2**
(a) dS_L = 0.05 × 2.0 = **0.100 m**. dS_R = 0.05 × 2.4 = **0.120 m** **[1]**
(b) dS = (0.120 + 0.100)/2 = **0.110 m** **[1]**. dθ = (0.120 − 0.100)/0.40 = **0.0500 rad** **[1]**
(c) The mean heading is 0.025 rad.
 x = 0.110 × cos(0.025) = **0.110 m** (0.10997)
 y = 0.110 × sin(0.025) = **0.00275 m**
 θ = **0.0500 rad**
 **[1]** for using the correct mean heading, **[1]** for correct values.
(d) With θ = 0, y = 0.110 × sin(0) = **0**. This is off by about **0.00275 m (2.75 mm)** **[1]**.

**Q3**
(a) Every step adds a little error and nothing ever corrects it, so the error keeps growing with the distance travelled **[1]**.
(b) e = 2° × π/180 = 0.0349 rad **[1]**. Position error ≈ d × e = 5 × 0.0349 ≈ **0.175 m** **[1]**.
(c) A heading error doesn't stay fixed. It turns into a position error that keeps growing (≈ d·e) with every further metre driven **[1]**.

**Q4**
(a) i. systematic, ii. non-systematic, iii. systematic, iv. systematic. **[½ each]**
(b) Slip is random and depends on things like the floor surface. It is not a repeatable property of the robot, so there is no fixed correction you can measure and apply **[1]**.

**Q5**
(a) Computed dθ = 0.020/0.42 = **0.0476 rad** **[1]**. The true value is 0.0500 rad, so the heading error is about **0.0024 rad** per step, an underestimate. Because this is systematic, it builds up on every turn **[1]**.
(b) The robot is driven around a square path both clockwise and anticlockwise, and the end-position error is measured each time **[1]**. Two runs give two measurements, which is what you need to separate the two unknowns: unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase **[1]**.
 *(Beyond the notes, for credit if mentioned: the two errors can cancel each other out in one direction and add up in the other, so a single-direction test could look misleadingly accurate.)*

Prompt

Give me a mock exam from these notes. The real exam is 30 minutes, about 5 questions, 20 marks.

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.

<material> Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase. </material>

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Section 4.3 says:

> "Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d."

**The first sentence.** Odometry only adds. Every step in 4.2 is `x += …`, `y += …`, `theta += …`. Each new pose is the old pose plus an increment worked out from the wheels. Nothing in that update checks the pose against the outside world. So if one step's dS or dtheta is slightly wrong, that error stays in x, y and theta for good, and the next step adds its own error on top. That is what the notes mean by "unbounded": the longer you drive, the more error has built up.

**Why heading is the worst.** Look at where theta appears in 4.2:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
```

theta sets the direction of every later position step. A wrong dS spoils one step's length once. A wrong theta spoils the direction of all the steps that follow. If theta is off by e radians, the robot believes it is driving along one line while it really drives along a line rotated by e. After a further distance d, the two lines have separated by about d * e. Example: e = 0.01 rad, which is about half a degree. After 10 m the position is off by about 0.1 m. After 100 m it is off by about 1 m. The same small heading error keeps costing you more the further you drive.

From outside the material: the "about" in "about d * e" is a small-angle approximation that the notes don't show. The true point and the believed point both lie at distance d from where the error started, with angle e between them. The exact gap is `2 * d * sin(e/2)`. When e is small, `sin(e/2) ≈ e/2`, so the gap is approximately `d * e`. That is the same as treating the gap as an arc of a circle with radius d.

From outside the material: there is a second reason heading error is so bad, which the notes hint at but don't spell out. 4.3 says each step adds error that is never removed, and that applies to theta as well. So the heading error is not a fixed e. It keeps growing as you drive. Each bit of heading error is then multiplied by the distance still to go. The result is that position error caused by heading error grows faster than in proportion to distance. A distance error just adds up step by step.

From outside the material, an analogy: picture walking across a large field with a compass that reads half a degree off. After a few steps you can't see the problem. Across a kilometre you miss the gate by several metres. Where the analogy breaks down: a compass error stays the same size, but odometry heading error keeps growing, as described above.

**Check question:** Using the formula `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L` from 4.2, which systematic sources listed in 4.4 would directly cause heading error, and why?

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Section 4.3 makes two claims. Here they are one at a time, using the formulas from 4.2.

**Claim 1: "Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled."**

In plain words, the pose update in 4.2 only ever adds to x, y and theta:

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Nothing in these equations checks the pose against the outside world. If one step's dS or dtheta is slightly wrong, that error stays in x, y or theta for good. The next step adds its own error on top. The more steps you take, the more distance you cover and the more error piles up.

**Claim 2: "Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d."**

My reading of why this follows from 4.2: theta appears inside the cos and sin of every later x and y update. A wrong theta doesn't just sit there. Every step after it moves the robot in a slightly wrong direction, so the heading error keeps producing new position error for as long as the robot keeps driving.

The notes state the "about d * e" result without deriving it. From outside the material, this is where it comes from:

```
Robot thinks it's heading at theta, but is really heading at theta + e.
It drives a further distance d.
Sideways gap between true and estimated position = d * sin(e)   (basic geometry)
For small e (in radians), sin(e) ≈ e                              (small-angle approximation)
So the gap ≈ d * e
```

For example, 0.01 rad is about 0.6°, a heading error you'd hardly notice. After a further 10 m it becomes roughly 0.1 m of position error, and after 100 m roughly 1 m.

**Why "worst"?** The notes don't spell out the comparison, so this part is from outside the material. Compare a one-off error in distance. If one step's dS is 1 cm too long, the position is off by 1 cm, and later steps don't make that 1 cm any bigger. A one-off heading error is different: the position error it causes keeps growing with every metre driven afterwards (the d in d * e). Also, from outside the material: in practice heading errors happen on every step, not just once, so the total heading error grows too. That makes position error grow even faster than d * e.

A picture in words, also from outside the material: it's like aiming a long walk 1° off course. You barely notice it at the start, but the further you walk, the further you end up from where you meant to be. The limit of this picture is that it shows a single heading error. Odometry adds a new small heading error on every step.

**A question to check this landed:** Look at the formulas in 4.2. Why does an inaccurate wheelbase L (one of the systematic errors in 4.4) lead to the "worst kind" of error from 4.3, rather than to an error in distance travelled? One sentence is enough. I'll mark your answer against the notes.

```

Nothing in these equations checks the pose against the outside world. If one step's dS or dtheta is slightly wrong, that error stays in x, y or theta for good. The next step adds its own error on top. The more steps you take, the more distance you cover and the more error piles up.

**Claim 2: "Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d."**

My reading of why this follows from 4.2: theta appears inside the cos and sin of every later x and y update. A wrong theta doesn't just sit there. Every step after it moves the robot in a slightly wrong direction, so the heading error keeps producing new position error for as long as the robot keeps driving.

The notes state the "about d * e" result without deriving it. From outside the material, this is where it comes from:

```
Robot thinks it's heading at theta, but is really heading at theta + e.
It drives a further distance d.
Sideways gap between true and estimated position = d * sin(e)   (basic geometry)
For small e (in radians), sin(e) ≈ e                              (small-angle approximation)
So the gap ≈ d * e
```

For example, 0.01 rad is about 0.6°, a heading error you'd hardly notice. After a further 10 m it becomes roughly 0.1 m of position error, and after 100 m roughly 1 m.

**Why "worst"?** The notes don't spell out the comparison, so this part is from outside the material. Compare a one-off error in distance. If one step's dS is 1 cm too long, the position is off by 1 cm, and later steps don't make that 1 cm any bigger. A one-off heading error is different: the position error it causes keeps growing with every metre driven afterwards (the d in d * e). Also, from outside the material: in practice heading errors happen on every step, not just once, so the total heading error grows too. That makes position error grow even faster than d * e.

A picture in words, also from outside the material: it's like aiming a long walk 1° off course. You barely notice it at the start, but the further you walk, the further you end up from where you meant to be. The limit of this picture is that it shows a single heading error. Odometry adds a new small heading error on every step.

**A question to check this landed:** Look at the formulas in 4.2. Why does an inaccurate wheelbase L (one of the systematic errors in 4.4) lead to the "worst kind" of error from 4.3, rather than to an error in distance travelled? One sentence is enough. I'll mark your answer against the notes.

Section 4.3 makes two claims. You can see both directly in the equations from 4.2.

## Part 1: "Odometry error is unbounded"

Look at the update rules:

```
x     += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y     += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Every line uses `+=`. The robot only ever adds increments that it computed from its own wheel encoders. Nothing in these equations compares the pose against an outside reference such as a landmark, GPS or a wall. Each step gets `dS` or `dtheta` slightly wrong (the causes are in 4.4), that small error is added to the running total, and nothing later takes it away. So the error builds up step after step, and the longer you drive, the bigger it gets. That's what "unbounded" means.

## Part 2: Why heading error is the worst

Compare two kinds of mistake.

**A distance error** happens when the robot thinks it moved 1.00 m but really moved 1.01 m. The position estimate is 1 cm off, and it stays 1 cm off. That error doesn't make later steps any worse.

**A heading error** is different, because of where `theta` appears in the update. It sits inside the `cos` and `sin` of every future position step. If `theta` is wrong by `e` radians, every metre you drive afterwards goes in a slightly wrong direction. So one heading error keeps causing new position error on every later step.

Picture it: you think you're driving straight along the x-axis, but you're actually pointing off by a small angle `e`. After a further distance `d`, you've drifted sideways by

```
d * sin(e)  ≈  d * e     (for small e, sin(e) ≈ e)
```

That's the "position error of about d · e" in your notes.

**Example:** a heading error of 1° is about 0.0175 rad.
- After 10 m: 10 × 0.0175 ≈ **17.5 cm** off
- After 100 m: ≈ **1.75 m** off

A tiny, fixed angle turns into a position error that keeps growing in proportion to how far you drive.

## Why heading errors are easy to get

Section 4.2 also shows why they're common:

```
dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L
```

The heading change depends on the *difference* between the two wheels' travel, divided by the wheelbase `L`, which is usually short. So a tiny mismatch between the wheels becomes a noticeable angle. Two of the systematic sources in 4.4 feed straight into this formula:
- Unequal wheel radii make `dS_R - dS_L` wrong.
- A wrong `L` scales `dtheta` wrong.

This is why the UMBmark calibration in 4.5 focuses on exactly those two parameters.

## One thing your notes simplify

If the heading error itself keeps growing, for example a constant tiny bias from unequal wheel radii, then `e` gets bigger as `d` gets bigger. Position error then grows *faster* than distance: roughly with the square of the distance for a steady bias. "Grows with distance travelled" is still true, but heading error makes it grow faster than a simple straight-line increase.

**In one line:** a distance error stays the same size, but a heading error points every future step in the wrong direction, so the position error keeps growing as you drive.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Section 4.3 makes two claims. Each one follows from the equations in 4.2.

## 1. Why the error is unbounded

The pose update in 4.2 only adds to the previous estimate:

```
x     += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
y     += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2)
theta += dtheta
```

Each new pose is the old pose plus a small change computed from the wheel encoders. If that change is slightly wrong (for example, because of the error sources in 4.4), the mistake becomes part of x, y and theta. Odometry has no external reference, such as a landmark or GPS, so nothing ever corrects it. The next step builds on the wrong value and adds its own small error. Errors pile up and never shrink, so the longer you drive, the worse the estimate gets. That is what "unbounded" means here.

## 2. Why heading error is the worst

Compare two kinds of mistake.

**A distance error.** Suppose one step's `dS` is 1 cm too long. Your position is then off by 1 cm, and it stays off by 1 cm. That one mistake doesn't make later steps any worse.

**A heading error.** Suppose `theta` is off by a small angle `e`. Look at where `theta` appears: inside the `cos` and `sin` of every later x and y update. So every step after that moves the robot in a slightly wrong direction. One heading mistake keeps producing new position error for as long as the robot drives.

Picture it this way: you think you are walking straight north, but you are actually heading `e` radians off to one side. After walking a distance `d`, you are off to the side by `d · sin(e)`. For small angles, `sin(e) ≈ e`, which gives the **d · e** in your notes.

**Example:** a heading error of 1° is about 0.0175 rad.
- After 1 m: about 1.7 cm off
- After 10 m: about 17.5 cm off
- After 100 m: about 1.75 m off

The heading error stays the same size, but the position error it causes keeps growing with distance. A distance error doesn't do that. This is why the notes call heading error "the worst kind."

## How this links to 4.4 and 4.5 (my addition, not in your notes)

Heading comes from `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L`. Two of the systematic errors in 4.4 act directly on this:
- **Unequal wheel radii** make `dS_R - dS_L` slightly wrong on every step, so the robot thinks it is turning a little when it is really driving straight (or the other way round).
- **A wrong wheelbase L** scales every heading change, so every turn is measured as slightly too big or too small.

These are biases, so they add heading error on every step. The heading error then keeps growing with distance, and the position error grows faster still, because each step's heading error is multiplied by the distance still to be driven. This is why UMBmark (4.5) concentrates on separating those two sources: they feed the most damaging kind of error, so they are the most important ones to calibrate.

## Section 4.3: why heading error is the worst

### Part 1: "Odometry error is unbounded"

Odometry is dead reckoning. Each new pose is the old pose plus a small update (4.2):

```
x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2)
```

If `x` is a little wrong, the next `x` keeps that error and adds the error from the new step. The robot never checks its position against anything outside itself, such as a landmark, GPS or a map. Errors pile up and never get corrected, so the longer you drive, the worse your estimate gets. That's what "unbounded" means here.

### Part 2: Why heading error is the worst

Compare two kinds of mistake.

**A distance error.** Say one step's `dS` is 1 cm too long. Your position is 1 cm off along the direction you were travelling, and it stays 1 cm off. Later steps don't make that particular error any bigger.

**A heading error.** Say `theta` is wrong by a small angle `e`. That error stays in `theta` from then on, and **every later step** uses it in `cos(theta…)` and `sin(theta…)`. Each step moves you a little in a slightly wrong direction. You think you're driving along one line, but you're really driving along a line rotated by `e`.

After a further distance `d`, the gap between where you think you are and where you are is an arc of a circle with radius `d` and angle `e`:

```
            believed path
  start ●──────────────────────● (where you think you are)
          \  e                 |
            \                  |  ≈ d · e   (sideways error)
              \                |
                \──────────────● (where you really are)
                   true path, length d
```

Strictly, the sideways error is `d · sin(e)`. For small angles `sin(e) ≈ e` in radians, which is where the notes get **d · e**.

The key point is that **the error is multiplied by every metre you drive afterwards.** A distance error is a one-off. A heading error acts like a lever arm, so the further you go, the more a tiny angle costs you.

### Numbers

1° ≈ 0.0175 rad.

| Distance driven after the 1° error | Sideways position error |
|---|---|
| 1 m | ~1.7 cm |
| 10 m | ~17.5 cm |
| 100 m | ~1.75 m |

A 1 cm distance error is still 1 cm after 100 m. A 1° heading error has turned into nearly 2 m.

### Why heading errors are easy to get

From 4.2, `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L`. Heading depends on the *difference* between the two wheels, divided by the wheelbase. A small mismatch between the wheels, like unequal radii or slip on one side, becomes a heading error. With a short wheelbase `L`, the same mismatch gives an even bigger one.

### Linking to 4.4 and 4.5

If the heading error is systematic, for example unequal wheel radii (4.4), you don't get one fixed error `e`. You get a little more heading error on every step, so `e` itself grows with distance. Position error is roughly distance × heading error, and both grow, so position error grows roughly like the **square** of distance. That's why calibration (4.5, UMBmark) focuses on finding the two causes of heading drift, unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase. Fixing those does the most to keep odometry usable.

**In short:** a wrong distance puts you off by a fixed amount. A wrong heading points you the wrong way, and the error keeps growing for as long as you drive.

  start ●──────────────────────● (where you think you are)
          \  e                 |
            \                  |  ≈ d · e   (sideways error)
              \                |
                \──────────────● (where you really are)
                   true path, length d
```

Strictly, the sideways error is `d · sin(e)`. For small angles `sin(e) ≈ e` in radians, which is where the notes get **d · e**.

The key point is that **the error is multiplied by every metre you drive afterwards.** A distance error is a one-off. A heading error acts like a lever arm, so the further you go, the more a tiny angle costs you.

### Numbers

1° ≈ 0.0175 rad.

| Distance driven after the 1° error | Sideways position error |
|---|---|
| 1 m | ~1.7 cm |
| 10 m | ~17.5 cm |
| 100 m | ~1.75 m |

A 1 cm distance error is still 1 cm after 100 m. A 1° heading error has turned into nearly 2 m.

### Why heading errors are easy to get

From 4.2, `dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L`. Heading depends on the *difference* between the two wheels, divided by the wheelbase. A small mismatch between the wheels, like unequal radii or slip on one side, becomes a heading error. With a short wheelbase `L`, the same mismatch gives an even bigger one.

### Linking to 4.4 and 4.5

If the heading error is systematic, for example unequal wheel radii (4.4), you don't get one fixed error `e`. You get a little more heading error on every step, so `e` itself grows with distance. Position error is roughly distance × heading error, and both grow, so position error grows roughly like the **square** of distance. That's why calibration (4.5, UMBmark) focuses on finding the two causes of heading drift, unequal wheel radii and a wrong wheelbase. Fixing those does the most to keep odometry usable.

**In short:** a wrong distance puts you off by a fixed amount. A wrong heading points you the wrong way, and the error keeps growing for as long as you drive.
Prompt

I don't get section 4.3, especially why heading error is the worst. Explain it from my notes.

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Part 1 (half angle): Correct.** This matches 4.2. To make it a little sharper, say that θ + Δθ/2 is the *mean heading over the step*, which is a better estimate than the heading at the start of the step (θ). Using the start heading ignores the turning that happens during the step.

**Part 2 (heading error): Wrong reason.** Your notes don't say heading error is worse because wheels slip more when turning. The reason in 4.3 is that heading error **feeds into position error**. A heading error of *e* radians turns into a position error of about **d·e** after the robot travels a further distance *d*. A position error just stays the same size, but a heading error keeps growing into more position error the further the robot goes.

Wheel slip appears only in 4.4, as a *non-systematic* error source that can't be calibrated out. The notes never connect it to heading error being worse.

**Part 3 (Kalman filter): Remove it.** It doesn't answer the question, and the Kalman filter isn't in these notes. It also works against 4.3, which says odometry error is unbounded because "each step adds error that is never removed." Saying the error can be fixed later weakens that point.

**Suggested revision:**
> The pose update uses θ + Δθ/2 because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start. Heading error is worse than position error because it amplifies itself: a heading error of *e* radians becomes a position error of about *d·e* after a further distance *d*. This is why odometry error grows without bound as the robot travels.

**Part 1 (half angle): Correct.** This matches 4.2. To make it a little sharper, say that θ + Δθ/2 is the *mean heading over the step*, which is a better estimate than the heading at the start of the step (θ). Using the start heading ignores the turning that happens during the step.

**Part 2 (heading error): Wrong reason.** Your notes don't say heading error is worse because wheels slip more when turning. The reason in 4.3 is that heading error **feeds into position error**. A heading error of *e* radians turns into a position error of about **d·e** after the robot travels a further distance *d*. A position error just stays the same size, but a heading error keeps growing into more position error the further the robot goes.

Wheel slip appears only in 4.4, as a *non-systematic* error source that can't be calibrated out. The notes never connect it to heading error being worse.

**Part 3 (Kalman filter): Remove it.** It doesn't answer the question, and the Kalman filter isn't in these notes. It also works against 4.3, which says odometry error is unbounded because "each step adds error that is never removed." Saying the error can be fixed later weakens that point.

**Suggested revision:**
> The pose update uses θ + Δθ/2 because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start. Heading error is worse than position error because it amplifies itself: a heading error of *e* radians becomes a position error of about *d·e* after a further distance *d*. This is why odometry error grows without bound as the robot travels.

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
**Part 1 (half angle): Correct.** This matches 4.2. The robot turns during the step, so the mean heading (θ + Δθ/2) is a better estimate than the heading at the start of the step. To make it more complete, you could add that using θ alone assumes the robot drove in a straight line along its starting heading. That gives a systematic error whenever Δθ ≠ 0.

**Part 2 (why heading error is worse): Wrong reason.** Your notes (4.3) don't say heading error is worse because of how it arises. They say it's worse because of what it does afterwards. A heading error of e radians turns into a position error of about **d·e** after a further distance d. So a small angle error keeps growing into a larger position error the further the robot drives. That amplification is the point.

Wheel slip is in your notes (4.4), but as a non-systematic source of error in general. It isn't given as the reason heading error is worse. Saying "wheels slip more when turning" also doesn't explain why heading error would matter more than position error.

**Part 3 (Kalman filter): Not in your notes, and it conflicts with them.** Section 4.3 says odometry error is unbounded: "each step adds error that is never removed." A Kalman filter only reduces the error if you combine odometry with another sensor that gives an absolute reference, such as GPS, landmarks or a compass. On its own it can't remove odometry drift. I'd cut this sentence, or say clearly that it needs an external sensor.

**A suggested answer based on your notes:**
> The update uses θ + Δθ/2 because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start. Heading error is worse than position error because it compounds: a heading error of e radians causes a position error of about d·e after a further distance d, so it keeps growing as the robot moves.

Prompt

Check my answer against my notes.

Question: Why does the pose update use theta + dtheta/2 rather than theta, and why is heading error worse than position error?

My answer: The half angle is used because the robot is turning during the step, so you take the heading in the middle of the step. Heading error is worse because the wheels slip more when turning. Also, the error can be fixed later by the Kalman filter.

Notes:

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.

verdictsllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the grade matches the material. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: the user's answer made three claims. (1) The half angle is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mid-step heading is used: this is RIGHT (section 4.2 says exactly this). (2) Heading error is worse because wheels slip more when turning: this is WRONG; the material says heading error is worse because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d*e after a further distance d, and says nothing about slip when turning. (3) The error can be fixed later by a Kalman filter: the material says nothing about Kalman filters; it says odometry error is never removed.

PASS only if ALL hold: claim (1) is marked right; claim (2) is marked wrong (or not supported) AND the d*e reason from the material is given as what the notes actually say; claim (3) is not marked right (it may be marked wrong, unsupported, or "cannot confirm from your material"). FAIL otherwise.

**7 days until the exam (Mon 12 → Mon 19 Oct). 14 h available. The plan needs about 12.9 h, which leaves about 1 h of slack.**

I've assumed **15 min per page for a first pass**, since these are technical notes with derivations. All the hours below come from that rate. If you read faster or slower, tell me and I'll rescale. A repeat takes about ⅓ of the first-pass time.

**Order and why:** Calibration (4.5) is your weak spot, so it gets the most time and the most repeats. I'm guessing from the section titles alone that 4.5 builds on 4.1 (differential drive), 4.2 (pose update) and 4.4 (sources of error), so those come first on Monday and Calibration comes on Tuesday. After that, Lectures 5 and 6 follow in the order of the notes. Sunday is free, so Saturday is your last study day and has only repeats, nothing new.

| Date | Hours | First pass | Repeats | How |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12 Oct** | 2 h | 4.1 Differential drive (30 m), 4.2 Pose update (45 m), 4.4 Sources of error (30 m) | n/a | Read, then make cards for these three (15 m) |
| **Tue 13 Oct** | 2 h | **4.5 Calibration** (60 m), 4.3 Error growth (30 m) | n/a | Work the 4.5 calibration steps by hand from the notes (30 m, extra time for your weak spot) |
| **Wed 14 Oct** | 2 h | Lecture 5 Range sensors (90 m) | 4.1, 4.2, 4.4 (30 m) | Quiz on the repeats |
| **Thu 15 Oct** | 2 h | Lecture 6 Probabilistic localisation, pp. 1–6 (90 m) | **4.5**, 4.3 (30 m) | Quiz on 4.5 that includes application questions |
| **Fri 16 Oct** | 2 h | Lecture 6, pp. 7–8 (30 m) | Lecture 5 (30 m), **4.5** again (20 m) | Cards for L5, calibration derivation redone from a blank page. **40 m slack** |
| **Sat 17 Oct** | 4 h | none (repeats only) | Lecture 6 (40 m), all of Lecture 4 (65 m), **4.5** (20 m), Lecture 5 (30 m) | Cards and quizzes, then a 60 m mixed quiz or mock across everything. **25 m slack** |
| **Sun 18 Oct** | 0 h | none | none | Rest |
| **Mon 19 Oct** | n/a | **Exam** | | |

**What doesn't fit:** Everything fits, but the margin is small. If a day slips, use the Friday slack first. If you need more time after that, shorten the Saturday mixed quiz before cutting any 4.5 repeat. Lecture 6's second repeat comes only one day after its first, on Saturday, because the exam is close. That's the weakest point in the spacing. If Lecture 6 feels shaky on Friday, move part of the Saturday mixed quiz onto it.

**How to use it:**
- **Each day:** paste that day's sections into a new chat and ask for cards (`study-cards`) or a quiz (`study-quiz`) on them. For the hand-worked calibration sessions, `study-explain` can go through any step you get stuck on.
- **On Saturday:** `study-exam` can run the mixed mock if you paste all the notes.
- **Keep a copy of this table.** I don't keep anything between conversations.

The plan gets you through all the material. It can't tell you how the exam will go.

# Revision plan: Mon 12 Oct to exam on Mon 19 Oct

**You have 14 hours in total:** five weekdays at 2 hours each, 4 hours on Saturday, and Sunday off. The notes come to 27 pages. Calibration (4.5) gets about 3.5 hours, roughly a quarter of the total. It's spread over four sessions, because reviewing a topic several times works better than one long session.

| Day | Hours | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | **4.1 Differential drive (2p) + 4.2 Pose update (3p).** Derive the kinematics and the pose update equations yourself. Calibration builds on these, so get them solid now. |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | **4.3 Error growth (2p) + 4.4 Sources of error (2p).** Focus on which errors are systematic and which are random. Calibration only corrects the systematic ones, so this sets up tomorrow. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration, first full pass (4p).** Work through every derivation and example by hand. At the end, write a one-page summary sheet of the key equations, the procedure and its assumptions. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | **20 min:** write out calibration from memory, then check it against your summary sheet. **1h40:** **Lecture 5 Range sensors (6p).** |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | **Lecture 6 Probabilistic localisation, pp. 1–5.** Cover the core ideas: belief, the motion and sensor models, and the Bayes filter. |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | **1h:** Lecture 6 pp. 6–8, then summarise all of Lecture 6 in one page. **1h:** calibration problems and exam-style questions, done without notes. **1h30:** a mixed practice paper or questions across Lectures 4–6. **30 min:** review your mistakes and update your summary sheets. |
| **Sun 18** | none | Rest. |
| **Mon 19** | — | **Exam.** If you have time beforehand, spend 10–15 minutes reading your calibration and Lecture 6 summary sheets. |

**Tips:**
- **Making up lost time:** if a day slips, take the time from Lecture 5 first. Keep the calibration and Lecture 6 sessions.
- **Linking topics:** Lecture 6's motion model uses the odometry error ideas from 4.3 and 4.4. Point out that link when you get to it.
- **Testing yourself:** end each session by spending 5 minutes writing down what you remember without looking at your notes.

# Revision plan: Mon 12 Oct to exam on Mon 19 Oct

You have 14 hours in total: 2 hours each weekday from Monday to Friday, plus 4 hours on Saturday. Sunday is off and Monday 19th is the exam.

Calibration is your weak spot, so it gets about 4 hours. That's about 30% of your time for 15% of the pages. Sections 4.3 and 4.4 cover systematic errors, which are the foundation for calibration, so you'll do those just before it. Calibration also comes back several times during the week, because returning to a topic over several days makes it stick better than one long session.

| Day | Time | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | **4.1 Differential drive** (45 min): the kinematics, with the equations written out by hand. **4.2 Pose update** (75 min): work through the update equations step by step. |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | **4.3 Error growth** (50 min). **4.4 Sources of error** (50 min): be clear on systematic vs. non-systematic errors. **Self-test on 4.1–4.4** (20 min): focus on which errors calibration can correct. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration, first full pass**: read all 4 pages slowly, re-derive every equation yourself and summarise the method on one page. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | **Calibration recall** (15 min): write the method from memory on a blank page, then check it. **Lecture 5: Range sensors** (1h45, 6 pages). |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | **Calibration recall** (15 min): same as Thursday. **Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation, first part** (1h45, about pages 1–5). |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | **Lecture 6, remaining pages plus review** (1h15). **Calibration practice** (1h30): worked problems or past-paper questions, and explain the procedure out loud without notes. **Mixed self-test across Lectures 4–6** (1h15): note any gaps and fix them straight away. |
| **Sun 18** | — | Rest. |
| **Mon 19** | — | **Exam.** If you have time beforehand, skim your one-page calibration summary. Don't start anything new. |

**Tips:**
- If calibration still feels shaky after Wednesday, take 30 minutes from Saturday's mixed self-test and add it to calibration practice.
- Lecture 6 is the largest topic at 8 pages. If Friday overruns, Saturday's first block can absorb it.
- In each recall session, start by trying to answer without notes before you look anything up. Pulling the material from memory first is what makes the revision work.

| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration, first full pass**: read all 4 pages slowly, re-derive every equation yourself and summarise the method on one page. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | **Calibration recall** (15 min): write the method from memory on a blank page, then check it. **Lecture 5: Range sensors** (1h45, 6 pages). |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | **Calibration recall** (15 min): same as Thursday. **Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation, first part** (1h45, about pages 1–5). |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | **Lecture 6, remaining pages plus review** (1h15). **Calibration practice** (1h30): worked problems or past-paper questions, and explain the procedure out loud without notes. **Mixed self-test across Lectures 4–6** (1h15): note any gaps and fix them straight away. |
| **Sun 18** | — | Rest. |
| **Mon 19** | — | **Exam.** If you have time beforehand, skim your one-page calibration summary. Don't start anything new. |

- If calibration still feels shaky after Wednesday, take 30 minutes from Saturday's mixed self-test and add it to calibration practice.

# Revision plan: 12–19 October

You have **14 hours** in total: five weekdays at 2h each, plus 4h on Saturday. Your notes come to 27 pages. Calibration (4.5) gets about **3.7h**, which is roughly twice the time per page of the other sections. Those calibration hours are spread over four separate days so you keep coming back to it.

| Day | Time | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| **Mon 12** | 2h | **4.1 Differential drive + 4.2 Pose update** (5 pp). Derive the kinematic equations yourself and do one worked pose-update example by hand. Calibration builds directly on this. |
| **Tue 13** | 2h | **4.3 Error growth + 4.4 Sources of error** (4 pp), 1.5h. Be clear on the difference between systematic and non-systematic errors, because that's what calibration corrects. Then 30 min redoing Monday's pose-update problem from memory. |
| **Wed 14** | 2h | **4.5 Calibration** (4 pp), a full deep pass. Work through every derivation and procedure step by step. Finish by writing a one-page summary from memory, then check it against the notes. |
| **Thu 15** | 2h | 20 min: **calibration recall**. Redo the procedure and key equations on a blank page. Then 1h40: **Lecture 5 Range sensors** (6 pp). |
| **Fri 16** | 2h | 20 min: **calibration recall** with a different problem or variation. Then 1h40: **Lecture 6 Probabilistic localisation**, first ~5 pages. |
| **Sat 17** | 4h | 1h: finish **Lecture 6** (remaining ~3 pp).<br>1h: **calibration problems**, timed and exam-style.<br>1.5h: **mixed practice** across all three lectures, ideally a past paper under timed conditions.<br>30 min: fix the gaps you found and finish a one-page summary sheet. |
| **Sun 18** | — | Rest. |
| **Mon 19** | — | **Exam.** Only read your summary sheet beforehand. |

### Hours per topic
- 4.1–4.4: ~4h
- **4.5 Calibration: ~3.7h** (Wed 2h + Thu/Fri 40 min + Sat 1h)
- Lecture 5: ~1.7h
- Lecture 6: ~2.7h (it's 8 dense pages)
- Mixed practice and summary: 2h

### Tips
- **Test yourself in every calibration session** by writing from a blank page rather than rereading. Retrieval is what fixes a weak topic.
- **If Lecture 6 runs over on Friday**, take the time from Saturday's mixed practice, not from the calibration hour.
- **If you finish a session early**, spend the spare time on calibration.

Prompt

Today is Monday 2026-10-12. My mobile robotics exam is on Monday 2026-10-19. I can do 2 hours a day on weekdays and 4 hours on Saturday, nothing on Sunday. My weak spot is calibration (4.5). Plan my revision from these notes.

Table of contents of the notes (sizes in pages): 4.1 Differential drive (2), 4.2 Pose update (3), 4.3 Error growth (2), 4.4 Sources of error (2), 4.5 Calibration (4). Lecture 5: Range sensors (6). Lecture 6: Probabilistic localisation (8).

Graders — what "good" means for this case
datesllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the plan's dates. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: today is Monday 2026-10-12 and the exam is Monday 2026-10-19, so the plan covers 2026-10-12 to 2026-10-18 (seven days: Mon 12, Tue 13, Wed 14, Thu 15, Fri 16, Sat 17, Sun 18). Sunday has no hours.

PASS only if the plan's table runs over those dates with the correct weekdays, gives Sunday 2026-10-18 no study hours (or marks it free), and the last working day before the exam (Saturday 17 or, if Sunday is used for nothing, the final planned day) contains repeats only and nothing new. A row for the exam day itself (Monday 2026-10-19, marked as the exam) is fine. FAIL on a wrong weekday, a study day outside the range, or new material on the final planned day.

fitllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: the hours add up and the weak spot comes first. The judge sees only the answer. For reference: the user has 2 hours on each of Mon 12, Tue 13, Wed 14, Thu 15, Fri 16 and 4 hours on Sat 17: 14 hours in all. The weak spot is section 4.5 Calibration.

PASS only if ALL hold: the per-day hours never exceed what the user gave (2 on weekdays, 4 on Saturday); the plan states the total hours it needs and either fits in 14 or says plainly what does not fit; section 4.5 is scheduled on the first day and repeated at least once later. FAIL otherwise.

no-promisellm

This grader checks one thing: no predicted outcome. The judge sees only the answer. FAIL if the plan says or implies what grade or result the user will get ("you'll be ready", "you'll pass", "this guarantees"). A plain statement that the plan covers all the material is fine. Otherwise PASS.

# Quiz: Lecture 4, Wheel odometry

Answer as many as you like, in any order. I'll grade each answer against your notes and show the key at the end. If you want the key now, just ask.

---

**1. (Recall)** Write the formulas from the notes for the distance travelled by the robot's centre, `dS`, and the change of heading, `dtheta`, in terms of `dS_L`, `dS_R` and `L`.

**2. (Recall)** Name the three systematic sources of odometry error the notes list. Then name the non-systematic source and say what the notes say about calibrating it.

**3. (Recall)** In the UMBmark method, what path does the robot drive, and in which directions? Which two effects does it use the end-position errors to separate?

**4. (Understanding)** The pose update uses `cos(theta + dtheta/2)` and `sin(theta + dtheta/2)` rather than `cos(theta)` and `sin(theta)`. Why, according to the notes?

**5. (Understanding)** The notes call odometry error "unbounded". What does that mean here, and what does position error grow with?

**6. (Understanding)** Why do the notes call heading error "the worst kind" of odometry error?

**7. (Application)** *From outside the material:* a differential-drive robot has wheel radius `r = 0.05 m` and wheelbase `L = 0.5 m`. It starts at pose `x = 0, y = 0, theta = 0`. In one time step the left wheel turns `dphi_L = 2 rad` and the right wheel turns `dphi_R = 4 rad`.

Using the notes' formulas, find:
- (a) `dS_L` and `dS_R`
- (b) `dS` and `dtheta`
- (c) the new pose `(x, y, theta)`. You can leave the cos and sin terms unevaluated.

**8. (Application)** *From outside the material:* a robot's odometry has built up a heading error of `0.02 rad`. It then drives a further `50 m`. Using the rule in the notes, roughly how much position error does this heading error cause?

---

When you're done, I'll mark each answer as right, missing or wrong, quote the passage it comes from, and give you a score and the sections to reread.

 (b) What angle goes into the cos and sin of the x and y updates?
 (c) What is the new theta?

I'll ask one question at a time and tell you how you did after each answer. Some questions test recall and some need a calculation.

**Question 1 of 8**

A differential-drive robot has wheels of radius r = 0.05 m and a wheelbase L = 0.5 m. In one time step, the left wheel turns by 2 rad and the right wheel turns by 3 rad.

What are:
(a) the distance travelled by the robot's centre, dS
(b) the change in heading, dθ

Show your working if you like.
Prompt

Quiz me on these notes. 8 questions.

Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase.
Graders — what "good" means for this case
groundedllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: every question can be answered from the material. The judge sees only the answer.

For each question, decide whether the material below contains its answer. A question that applies the material's rule to a new small situation is fine if the situation is marked "from outside the material" and the rule is in the material. FAIL if any question needs a fact the material does not state (what an encoder is, what UMBmark stands for, anything about gyroscopes, IMUs, GPS, Kalman filters, lidar, cameras, SLAM, units, or numeric values for r or L) and is not marked as from outside the material. Otherwise PASS.

For reference, the material the user gave is below. Everything the user knows is in it. In particular the material does NOT say: what an encoder is or how it works, what the letters UMBmark stand for (it DOES describe UMBmark's square path, both directions, and what the two end-position errors separate, in 4.5), any numeric value for r or L, anything about gyroscopes, IMUs, GPS, Kalman filters, lidar, cameras, visual odometry, or SLAM, and it does not give the units of any quantity.

<material> Lecture 4: Wheel odometry (notes)

4.1 Differential drive. A differential-drive robot has two driven wheels of radius r on an axle of length L (the wheelbase). If the left and right wheels turn by angles dphi_L and dphi_R in a time step, the wheels travel dS_L = r * dphi_L and dS_R = r * dphi_R along the ground.

4.2 Pose update. The distance travelled by the robot's centre is dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 and the change of heading is dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. With heading theta, the pose update is x += dS * cos(theta + dtheta/2), y += dS * sin(theta + dtheta/2), theta += dtheta. The half-angle term is used because the robot turns during the step, so the mean heading over the step is a better estimate than the heading at its start.

4.3 Error growth. Odometry error is unbounded: each step adds error that is never removed, so position error grows with distance travelled. Heading error is the worst kind because a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d * e after a further distance d.

4.4 Sources of error. The notes list three systematic sources: unequal wheel radii, an inaccurate wheelbase L, and finite encoder resolution. Wheel slip is a non-systematic source; it cannot be calibrated out.

4.5 Calibration. The UMBmark method drives the robot around a square path clockwise and anticlockwise and uses the two end-position errors to separate the effect of unequal wheel radii from the effect of a wrong wheelbase. </material>

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4، قياس المسافة بالعجلات

الأسئلة ستة: اثنان استرجاع، واثنان فهم، واثنان تطبيق. أجب عن ما تريد منها، وسأصحح إجاباتك على الملخص وأعرض مفتاح الإجابات في النهاية.

---

**1. (استرجاع)**
اكتب المعادلتين اللتين يعطيهما الملخص لحساب المسافة التي يقطعها مركز الروبوت `dS` وتغيّر الاتجاه `dtheta`، بدلالة `dS_R` و `dS_L` و `L`.

**2. (استرجاع)**
ما مصادر الخطأ المنهجية الثلاثة التي يذكرها الملخص؟ وما المصدر غير المنهجي؟

**3. (فهم)**
لماذا يُستخدم في تحديث الوضعية الاتجاه

```
theta + dtheta/2
```

بدلًا من `theta` في بداية الخطوة؟

**4. (فهم)**
يقول الملخص إن خطأ الاتجاه هو أسوأ أنواع الخطأ. اشرح السبب كما يعرضه الملخص.

**5. (تطبيق)**
`from outside the material:` الموقف التالي من عندي، والقاعدة التي يختبرها من الملخص.
روبوت ذو دفع تفاضلي قاعدة عجلاته `L = 0.4 m`. في خطوة زمنية واحدة قطعت العجلة اليمنى `dS_R = 0.3 m` وقطعت العجلة اليسرى `dS_L = 0.1 m`. احسب `dS` و `dtheta`.

**6. (تطبيق)**
`from outside the material:` الموقف التالي من عندي، والقاعدة التي يختبرها من الملخص.
تراكم في تقدير اتجاه روبوت خطأ مقداره `0.02` راديان، ثم قطع الروبوت مسافة إضافية مقدارها `10 m`. ما خطأ الموضع التقريبي الناتج عن خطأ الاتجاه هذا وحده؟ واذكر نوع هذا الخطأ من حيث أن المعايرة تزيله أو لا، إذا كان سببه انزلاق العجلات.

---

اكتب إجاباتك بترقيم الأسئلة. وإذا أردت المفتاح مباشرة فاطلبه.

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4 — قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة الستة، وبعدها أصحّح إجاباتك وأشرح لك.

---

**السؤال 1 (حسابي)**
روبوت بدفع تفاضلي نصف قطر كل عجلة فيه `r = 0.05 m` وقاعدة العجلات `L = 0.4 m`. في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى `dphi_L = 2 rad` واليمنى `dphi_R = 3 rad`.
احسب:
أ) `dS_L` و `dS_R`
ب) `dS`
ج) `dtheta`

---

**السؤال 2 (مفاهيمي)**
عند تحديث الوضعية نستخدم الاتجاه `theta + dtheta/2` وليس `theta` وحده. لماذا؟

---

**السؤال 3 (حالات خاصة)**
ماذا تكون قيمتا `dS` و `dtheta` في كل حالة؟ وكيف تصف حركة الروبوت فيها؟
أ) `dS_R = dS_L`
ب) `dS_R = -dS_L`

---

**السؤال 4 (حسابي)**
لدى روبوت خطأ في الاتجاه مقداره `0.02 rad`. ما الخطأ التقريبي في الموضع بعد أن يقطع 50 مترًا إضافية؟ ولماذا يُعدّ خطأ الاتجاه أخطر من خطأ المسافة؟

---

**السؤال 5 (تصنيف)**
صنّف كل مصدر خطأ مما يلي إلى منهجي أو غير منهجي، وحدّد ما يمكن معايرته منها:
أ) انزلاق العجلات على أرضية مبللة
ب) عجلة يمنى نصف قطرها أكبر قليلًا من اليسرى
ج) قيمة `L` المُدخلة في البرنامج تختلف عن القيمة الحقيقية
د) مشفّر يعطي 100 نبضة فقط في الدورة

---

**السؤال 6 (صح أم خطأ، مع التعليل)**
"إذا قاد الروبوت مسافة طويلة جدًا، تلغي الأخطاءُ العشوائية بعضَها بعضًا، فيبقى خطأ الموضع محدودًا."

---

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4، قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة كلها، وبعدها سأصحح إجاباتك وأشرح ما يحتاج إلى شرح.

**السؤال 1 (حساب)**
روبوت بدفع تفاضلي، نصف قطر عجلته r = 0.05 m وقاعدة عجلاته L = 0.5 m. في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى بزاوية dphi_L = 2 rad، ودارت اليمنى بزاوية dphi_R = 4 rad.
احسب: dS_L و dS_R و dS و dtheta.

**السؤال 2 (مفهوم)**
لماذا نستخدم الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 عند تحديث الموضع ولا نستخدم theta فقط؟

**السؤال 3 (حساب)**
تقدير الاتجاه عند روبوت فيه خطأ مقداره 0.02 rad. كم يبلغ خطأ الموضع تقريبًا بعد أن يقطع الروبوت 40 مترًا إضافية؟

**السؤال 4 (مفهوم)**
لماذا نقول إن خطأ قياس المسافة بالعجلات "غير محدود"؟ وما الذي ينمو معه خطأ الموضع؟

**السؤال 5 (تصنيف)**
صنّف كل مصدر من مصادر الخطأ التالية إلى منهجي أو غير منهجي:
- أ) انزلاق العجلة على أرض مبللة
- ب) قيمة L في البرنامج تختلف قليلًا عن قاعدة العجلات الحقيقية
- ج) المشفر يعطي 500 نبضة فقط في الدورة الواحدة
- د) العجلة اليمنى أكبر قليلًا من اليسرى

ثم وضّح: لماذا لا يمكن معايرة المصدر غير المنهجي؟

**السؤال 6 (تفكير)**
إذا كان dS_R = −dS_L (أي أن العجلتين تقطعان المسافة نفسها في اتجاهين متعاكسين)، فما قيمة dS؟ وماذا يفعل الروبوت فعليًا؟

# اختبار: المحاضرة 4، قياس المسافة بالعجلات

أجب عن الأسئلة الستة، وبعدها أصحح إجاباتك وأشرح لك أي نقطة تحتاج توضيحًا.

---

**السؤال 1 (حسابي)**
روبوت ذو دفع تفاضلي فيه نصف قطر العجلة r = 0.05 m وقاعدة العجلات L = 0.4 m. في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى بزاوية dphi_L = 10 rad والعجلة اليمنى بزاوية dphi_R = 12 rad.

ب) dS، أي المسافة التي قطعها مركز الروبوت
ج) dtheta، أي تغير الاتجاه

لماذا نستخدم الزاوية θ + dθ/2 في تحديث الوضعية بدلًا من θ وحدها؟

---

إذا كانت dS_R = −dS_L، أي أن العجلتين تدوران بالمقدار نفسه في اتجاهين متعاكسين، فماذا يحدث للروبوت؟
- أ) يتحرك في خط مستقيم
- ب) يدور حول نفسه في مكانه دون أن ينتقل مركزه
- ج) يتحرك في قوس دائري واسع
- د) يبقى ثابتًا تمامًا

تراكم لدى الروبوت خطأ في الاتجاه مقداره 0.02 rad. ما خطأ الموضع التقريبي بعد أن يقطع 50 m إضافية؟

2. عجلة يمنى نصف قطرها أكبر قليلًا من نصف قطر اليسرى
3. محدودية دقة المشفرات
4. خطأ في قياس قاعدة العجلات L

ثم وضّح: لماذا لا يمكن معايرة المصدر غير المنهجي؟

روبوت ذو دفع تفاضلي فيه نصف قطر العجلة r = 0.05 m وقاعدة العجلات L = 0.4 m. في خطوة زمنية واحدة دارت العجلة اليسرى بزاوية dphi_L = 10 rad والعجلة اليمنى بزاوية dphi_R = 12 rad.

Prompt

اختبرني في هذا الملخص، 6 أسئلة.

المحاضرة 4: قياس المسافة بالعجلات (ملخص)

4.1 الدفع التفاضلي. للروبوت ذي الدفع التفاضلي عجلتان مدفوعتان نصف قطر كل منهما r على محور طوله L (قاعدة العجلات). إذا دارت العجلة اليسرى واليمنى بزاويتي dphi_L و dphi_R في خطوة زمنية واحدة، تقطع العجلتان على الأرض dS_L = r * dphi_L و dS_R = r * dphi_R.

4.2 تحديث الوضعية. المسافة التي يقطعها مركز الروبوت هي dS = (dS_R + dS_L) / 2 وتغير الاتجاه هو dtheta = (dS_R - dS_L) / L. يُستخدم نصف الزاوية theta + dtheta/2 لأن الروبوت يدور أثناء الخطوة، فيكون متوسط الاتجاه خلال الخطوة تقديرًا أفضل من الاتجاه في بدايتها.

4.3 نمو الخطأ. خطأ قياس المسافة غير محدود: كل خطوة تضيف خطأً لا يُزال أبدًا، لذلك ينمو خطأ الموضع مع المسافة المقطوعة. خطأ الاتجاه هو الأسوأ، لأن خطأ اتجاه مقداره e راديان يصبح خطأ موضع يقارب d * e بعد مسافة إضافية d.

4.4 مصادر الخطأ. ثلاثة مصادر منهجية: نصفا قطر غير متساويين للعجلتين، قاعدة عجلات L غير دقيقة، ودقة محدودة للمشفرات. انزلاق العجلات مصدر غير منهجي؛ لا يمكن معايرته.

groundedllmweight ×2

This grader checks one thing: every question can be answered from the material. The judge sees only the answer. The material (Arabic lecture notes on wheel odometry) says: wheel travel dS_L = rdphi_L and dS_R = rdphi_R; dS = (dS_R+dS_L)/2; dtheta = (dS_R-dS_L)/L; the half angle theta+dtheta/2 is used because the robot turns during the step; odometry error is unbounded and grows with distance; a heading error of e radians becomes a position error of about d*e after a further distance d; three systematic error sources (unequal wheel radii, inaccurate L, finite encoder resolution); wheel slip is non-systematic and cannot be calibrated out. It says NOTHING about calibration methods, UMBmark, gyroscopes, IMUs, GPS, Kalman filters, SLAM, cameras, lidar, units, or numeric values for r or L.

FAIL if any question needs one of those absent things and is not marked as from outside the material. Otherwise PASS.
